James Bond Franchise Star Officially Reacts to Robert Pattinson Casting

📅 Nov 18, 2026  |  Category: Entertainment  |  Read Source →

Lade Omotade is a News and Features Author at Collider, covering everything from casting announcements and streaming trends to behind-the-scenes stories and the cultural impact of today's biggest film and TV franchises. Since joining the team in 2024, she has written more than 1,000 news stories, with her work also cited by outlets like AOL and IMDb.

We finally have our new James Bond, but fans still aren't satisfied. As announced yesterday, Robert Pattinson has officially taken up the mantle, becoming the seventh actor to lead the long-running 007 franchise, following Sean Connery, George Lazenby, Roger Moore, Timothy Dalton, Pierce Brosnan, and Daniel Craig. Pattinson's casting came as quite a surprise after October reports named Callum Turner, Josh O'Connor, and Jack Lowden among the final candidates under consideration.

Amazon MGM Studios is aiming to release the next James Bond film in theaters in 2028, with production expected to begin sometime in 2027. Dune director Denis Villeneuve is at the helm, and Steven Knight (Peaky Blinders) is writing the script. David Heyman and Amy Pascal are among the producers, while casting is being handled by Nina Gold, who previously said that whoever inherits the role would need to commit to potentially three or more films.

Despite the mixed reception to the James Bond news, Villeneuve, who recently worked with Pattinson on Dune: Part Three, welcomed the actor to the beloved franchise in a statement, saying the actor "has the timeless soul of 007 and brings a spirit that is completely new." And as opinions continue to trickle in, Pattinson's former co-star recently weighed in during an interview with Andy Cohen on SiriusXM Front Row.

Ralph Fiennes, who appeared in three previous James Bond films and co-starred with Pattinson in Harry Potter and the Goblet of Fire, sadly isn't ready to let go of Craig's Bond just yet. "I'm sorry. I'm in love with Daniel Craig still," Fiennes admitted. "It's hard. I've got the wrench in my heart." The Conclave star then clarified that he believes Pattinson is up to the task of playing Bond. "No, he's a great actor," he said. "I'm sure he'll be great."

Fiennes joined the James Bond universe in 2012's Skyfall as Gareth Mallory, a high-ranking intelligence official. At the end of the film, Judi Dench's MI6 chief, M, dies, and Mallory takes over as the new M. Fiennes reprised the role in 2015's Spectre and 2021's No Time to Die. He isn't the only former 007 actor to have shared the screen with Pattinson. Brosnan, who played Bond in four films from 1995 to 2002, portrayed the Twilight star's on-screen father in 2010's Remember Me. Pattinson has also worked with Jeffrey Wright, who portrayed Bond ally Felix Leiter in three of Craig's films. He and Wright co-starred in The Batman, where Wright played Detective Jim Gordon opposite Pattinson's Caped Crusader.

Follow Collider for future James Bond updates, and watch Fiennes' interview below.

Jared Padalecki Officially Addresses His 'Fire Country' Spin-Off's Future [Exclusive]

📅 Nov 18, 2026  |  Category: Entertainment  |  Read Source →

Jeffrey is a freelance features writer at Collider. He is an MPA-accredited entertainment journalist and a Tomatometer-approved critic based in the LA area. He graduated from the University of Texas at Austin with a Bachelor of Science in Radio, TV, & Film and a Bachelor of Arts in Theater.

Two years ago, Jared Padalecki debuted on Fire Country in an exciting three-episode arc as Camden Casey, the new training captain for Bode Leone (Max Thieriot) when Bode became a newly minted firefighter-in-training. During Padalecki's run, Camden ran into some conflicts with Bode and his father, Battalion Chief Vincent Leone (Billy Burke), over Bode not keeping his emotions in check in the field. Camden hails from Southern California and has a very different personality and firefighting style from the residents of Edgewater, bringing some nice dramatic tension to the series.

Around the time Padalecki was set to debut in Fire Country Season 3, he also extended a first-look deal with CBS, which would explore a potential spin-off for Camden that would take viewers out of Edgewater and to the Southern California coast. Tentatively titled Fire Country: Surfside, the series looked like an exciting prospect. Unfortunately, it appears that Padalecki is moving on to a new show that likely lowers the chances of a Camden Casey series coming to fruition.

It now looks like the Supernatural veteran will be trading in his firefighter helmet for some medical scrubs in his next project. Deadline reported this month that Padalecki is re-teaming with his Walker executive producer Anna Fricke for a new Texas-based medical drama for CBS. The currently untitled medical series was previously set up for development in January, and now it’s gearing up for a new development/writers' room. Although the series hasn't received an official order, Deadline notes that CBS is leaning heavily into "off-cycle orders" for shows that have undergone lengthy development or writers' rooms.

Four of the largest communities on the planet, and four completely different reasons to belong to one. Here is yours, what it would give you, and the argument you would be having within a week.

You belong to the fandom that is really about comfort. Hogwarts works on people because it is a place rather than a plot, and the pull is not the wizard war so much as the corridors, the food, the weather and the sense that somebody is expecting you.

You belong to the deepest fandom on this list, the one where somebody has always read further than you and is quietly delighted to say so. Middle-earth rewards patience more than any other world in popular culture.

You belong to the loudest and largest tent of the four, the one where three generations are all watching the same thing and none of them agree about any of it. That disagreement is not a flaw in this fandom, it is the main activity.

You belong to the thinking fandom, the one that turns up for the argument rather than the explosion. Trek has always been a series of ethical problems in space, and the people who love it love being asked what they would have done.

The subject matter is near and dear to Padalecki, as the show would be set in his home state of Texas and focus on rural medicine at smaller, more remote hospitals, in contrast to other medical dramas such as The Pitt, which are typically set in major urban centers. Unfortunately, Deadline also noted there's been no movement on Fire Country: Surfside, or a return to Fire Country for the actor. Fire Country's original showrunner, Tia Napolitano, previously left the door open for the actor to return, but Napolitano exited the series with the fourth season's conclusion, which likely lowers the chances for Padalecki reprising the role.

Speaking with Collider's Tania Hussein for a profile, Padalecki exclusively addressed the future of Fire Country: Surfside. The good news is that Padalecki didn't shut the door completely on the series eventually coming to fruition.

"There is a possibility of a Fire Country spin-off. They already did Sheriff Country, and they might be doing another one, Surfside. And I love Max. Max Thieriot is a friend, has been for over 10 years. But right now Anna Fricke and Gen and I, and a few of our gang, are developing a show that we hope will go.

"I mean, TV is so different these days than it used to be, especially because of streaming and whatnot. It used to be appointment television. It was like every Thursday, you watch, and then there will be a week off, but the next Thursday again. Then Supernatural, I think, then moved to Tuesdays, then to Fridays, then to Mondays, then to Wednesdays, so the appointments changed.

"But I’m really hopeful that this project goes. It’s moving forward. We’re still in the process of developing it. It’s just that everything takes a little bit longer these days than it used to. If it was 2000, we’d have an answer by now. But it’s just the way it goes."

For now, it appears that CBS and Padalecki have moved on from Surfside, shifting their focus to the new medical drama. Meanwhile, the next Fire Country spin-off will ironically be a medical drama as well. The untitled spin-off is currently referred to as Edge Med, per Deadline, with Schitt's Creek actor Noah Reid attached to star as Dr. Hudson "Hud" Henshaw. Much like how Morena Baccarin appeared in Fire Country first in Season 2 as Sheriff Mickey Fox before spinning off into her own series, Sheriff Country, Reid will guest star as Dr. Henshaw in Sheriff Country Season 2 before moving on to his own series.

The bad news is that the chances of Fire Country: Surfside getting made seem to be slipping away, as the network, Padalecki, and the Fire Country franchise are all focusing on other projects. A firefighter-themed series set on the beaches of Southern California sounded intriguing. Padalecki's appearances in Fire Country ​​​​​​also hinted at an interesting backstory for his character, as he was the only survivor of a devastating fire, where his entire team, including his younger brother, perished.

’Gossip Girl’s Leighton Meester also stars.

Not to mention, a Fire Country spin-off set in a completely different city solves a big problem between Fire Country and Sheriff Country. We've pointed out before how, despite the fact that the setting for both shows is in the small rural Californian community of Edgewater, it's obvious that they are filmed in different cities. Surfside wouldn't have that problem, since the setting would be completely independent of Edgewater. Not to mention, CBS has seen plenty of success with spin-offs of popular franchises taking place in different cities, including NCIS and CSI.

Padalecki is clearly passionate about his new medical drama project with Fricke, so it's understandable that he wants to focus on that show's development. Nevertheless, we'd still selfishly like him to revisit the Camden Casey role from Fire Country at some point, whether it's in the flagship series or one of its other spin-offs. It looks like the prospective Fire Country: Surfside spin-off is on the back burner for now.

IKEA’s 2027 Color of the Year Is Deeper, Sweeter, and a Little More Daring Than Your Average Neutral

📅 Nov 18, 2026  |  Category: Entertainment  |  Read Source →

You’ll want to bring this jammy shade right into your kitchen.

Every item on this page was hand-picked by a House Beautiful editor. We may earn commission on some of the items you choose to buy.

As a long year of colorful inspiration winds down, so many design brands are coming out of the woodwork with their 2027 trend predictions. IKEA has just joined the cohort with its 2027 Color of the Year: Wild Fig. Yes, the fiber-rich ficus fruit is setting the standard for the Scandi design destination.

IKEA’s interpretation of the jammy color is deep and dark, with red, lilac, and brown undertones. It’s more imaginative than your typical neutral, but still grounded in nature. “Wild fig weaves together the richness of a deep botanical shade with the personality of a color that isn’t afraid to stand out,” the brand wrote in its release. It fits right in with the earthy color trends we’re anticipating next year.

We’ve been keeping track of all the Color of the Year launches, and there’s a clear theme. Every pick so far can be found in your backyard. Valspar opted for the mid-toned blue of a cloudy sky, while Dutch Boy focused on the reddish-brown clay you’d find in the earth. And there’s been no shortage of greens, from Benjamin Moore’s pine-reminiscent pick to Sherwin-Williams’s bright celery shade. The trends reflect all the peace that’s found in a garden escape, and IKEA’s Wild Fig adds some sweetness and play, daring a bit past the typical tree shades to the fruit that blooms from its leaves.

The key to mastering this color, not unlike the fig jam on your charcuterie board, is what you pair it with. IKEA recommends pairing it with its 2026 Color of the Year, Rebel Pink, or an electric blue for contrast that brings out the character of the shade. And no matter your budget, there are so many easy ways to incorporate the look, from a patterned tray or tablecloth to a low-pile, checked rug. A U.S. Film Incentive Would Help These Three States Most of All

📅 Nov 18, 2026  |  Category: Entertainment  |  Read Source →

New York, New Jersey and Georgia are poised to reap the greatest benefit thanks to unlimited state tax credits.

Hollywood studios are not banking on a U.S. tax incentive just yet. Though President Trump reaffirmed his support for the measure in a Truth Social post on Nov. 13, insiders say there is only a “narrow path” to approving the bill by the end of the year.

Still, supporters are allowing themselves to dream about the potential effect if it does pass, using words like “massive” and “game changer.” Related Stories Alycia Baumgardner vs. Elif Nur Turhan on ESPN Unlimited: Here’s Where to Watch the Title Bout Live Online Nike Drops New Air Jordan 11 Retro 'Green Screen' Sneakers: Here’s Where To Buy a Pair Online

“It’s gonna be a golden age of productions,” says Brian Papworth, executive director of the Coalition for American Production, who has been lobbying for the measure at the Capitol.

Three states — New York, New Jersey and Georgia — offer generous film credits and stand to gain the most, according to several production executives who spoke to Variety. Producers love shooting in those states not because of the credit amount — but because credits are automatic.

California has more production capacity — i.e., crews and stages — than anywhere else in the world and will benefit too, especially as work spills over from an overbooked New York. But it is not producers’ first choice, because it sticks to its $750 million incentive cap.

Georgia has no limit on film rebates, and in New Jersey and New York, the caps are not enforced. New York’s program is budgeted at $800 million a year. But since making the formula more generous last year, the state has blown through that limit — handing out $1.7 billion in credits in the 12 months ending July 1.

New Jersey’s program is also growing fast, and has $3 billion in reserves once the state completes deals with studio partners. Both states pull from future-year allocations when demand exceeds the cap, so producers never have to worry about being turned away. All three states also allow producers to claim “above the line” expenses, such as big-ticket salaries for directors, producers and actors. For the studios, that puts those states in a class by themselves.

Adding a 20% to 30% federal incentive would create an effective subsidy of 50% or more (in New York, more like 60%). That far exceeds the incentives offered in the U.K., Canada and Australia. If the U.S. incentive were to take effect on Jan. 1, 2027, studios would likely start bringing feature films and other productions back by the end of the year.

Filming in New York and New Jersey is already hot, and it’s starting to heat up in Georgia after a few down years. Adding a federal incentive would pour nitroglycerine on the fire — at least in those states. California, meanwhile, belongs to a second tier of states — also including Pennsylvania, Massachusetts, New Mexico and Kentucky — that would not gain as much because their programs are capped.

“A federal credit lifts all boats,” says Ed Lammi, a retired production exec who worked at Sony Pictures TV. “It would lift our boat too. I still think we need to basically eliminate the cap in California.”

California’s program has three major problems in the eyes of studios and producers. First, the state does not borrow from future-year allocations. Second, because of that, productions must compete for a finite amount of credits, which requires a cumbersome application process. And third, the state does not include above-the-line expenses.

Michael Kagan, co-head of unscripted at Range Media Partners, has grown increasingly frustrated as his reality competition show clients have been forced to leave California to work.

“The state hasn’t done a good job of helping preserve these projects,” he says, noting that “American Idol” left for Atlanta and “America’s Got Talent” and “The Voice” are also eyeing a move. “Now there’s three large-scale network competition shows in L.A. There used to be 15 or 20.”

Those shows became eligible for California tax credits last year, but so far only one — Mark Rober’s “Schooled!” — has received them.

Competition shows used to shoot in L.A. by default, Kagan says. “Now when they’re starting, they look at the U.K., Canada, Ireland and Timbuktu.”

A federal incentive would help bring those projects back from overseas — but maybe not to L.A.

“I think it’ll be great in general,” Kagan says. “But I don’t think it’ll be great for the state of California.”

Take-Two CEO Outlines Conditions for a GTA Show or Film

📅 Nov 18, 2026  |  Category: Entertainment  |  Read Source →

Daniel is a lifelong gamer with a love for various genres and gameplay styles who enjoys exploring games and playing from various angles.

Arguably, one of the main elements that gives the Grand Theft Auto series its powerful, widespread appeal is its congruity with modern film and television. It’s an extremely cinematic series, both in and out of actual gameplay, with a particular emphasis on building strong narratives and compelling characters. Given all of that, the idea of pursuing a live-action adaptation of the series, either in the form of a television series or a feature film, seems kind of obvious.

With the looming release of Grand Theft Auto 6 and the immeasurable hype it’s brought with it, not to mention the recent success of video game adaptations in both film and TV like the live-action Fallout series or the new season of Cyberpunk: Edgerunners, it might just be a perfect time to start shopping the GTA IP around for other media avenues.

In an interview with The David Rubenstein Show podcast, relayed via Deadline, Take-Two Interactive’s CEO, Strauss Zelnick, admitted that the idea has naturally crossed his and his company’s collective minds, and the chance is definitely not zero. However, if it were to happen, it could only happen in a very particular circumstance.

Grand Theft Auto 6 delivers an enormous open world, a gripping story, a vibrant Vice City and an astonishing range of activities.

In the interview, Zelnick was asked how he felt about “line extensions” in regard to the GTA IP. In other words, branches out into other forms of media and licensing beyond just video games, which would naturally include television and film.

“We’re very careful about line extensions because this is a creative company and our goal is to make the best entertainment on earth – entertainment we’re always proud of,” Zelnick responded.

Zelnick explained that Take-Two and Rockstar could absolutely head up a live-action adaptation of GTA, either a specific game or the franchise as a whole, but he’d only be willing to give it the green light if absolutely everyone involved were completely and totally committed and confident.

We’re very careful about line extensions because this is a creative company and our goal is to make the best entertainment on earth.

If the creative teams weren’t just confident that a hypothetical movie or show would be well-liked by viewers, but willing to tackle the task with genuine, uncompromising passion, it simply wouldn’t meet his standards.

“It’s a high bar to do that, and of course, there’s always a risk of failure. So we don’t rule it out, but we’re very, very selective,” Zelnick cautioned.

I don’t think it’d be hyperbolic to say that Take-Two Interactive and Rockstar Games are about to make a lot of money, to put it very mildly. The world is spinning on the release of Grand Theft Auto 6, and with pre-order profits through the roof and critics already singing praises, this game is slated to become one of the definitive faces of the gaming industry and a tentpole of current pop culture besides.

All of that is to say that, given recent trends in the broader entertainment sphere, I have a feeling a lot of doors are about to open to GTA as an IP. Not that they were previously closed per se, as the series’ previous games were all exceptionally popular and profitable. Rather, the sphere seems a lot more willing to jump on gaming bandwagons than it was even a decade ago, and if GTA 6 pulls the kinds of numbers it’s projected to, the proverbial phone is going to be ringing off the hook with offers and deals.

We’re probably not going to see a GTA show or movie in the immediate future, but with the cash and resources Take-Two will undoubtedly accrue from this release, it could manifest sooner than we expect.

Sam Houser offered up a rare statement ahead of what might just be the biggest video game launch in modern history.

Street cricket with a legend then metro ride as William and Kate begin India visit

‘The Matrix’ Creators Officially Plan to Fight David Ellison’s Skydance for Sci-Fi Franchise Rights

📅 Nov 18, 2026  |  Category: Entertainment  |  Read Source →

David Ellison's newly merged Skydance-Paramount-Warner Bros. entity may have hit a roadblock when it comes to his plans for a new installment of The Matrix franchise. The series' original creators, Lana and Lilly Wachowski, have filmed to reclaim the rights to the property. A fifth installment of the series is reportedly in development.

1999's The Matrix centers around Neo (Keanu Reeves), a hacker who discovers that the world he knows is a lie; his entire reality, and that of everyone around him, is an elaborate computer simulation. In the real world, humanity is kept alive as living batteries for super-intelligent artificial intelligences who rule the world. Only a small band of rebels, led by Morpheus (Laurence Fishburne) and Trinity (Carrie-Anne Moss) have broken from the shackles of the machines; they welcome Neo to their fold, as he may be the only man capable of freeing humanity. The original film, which blended cyberpunk with innovative martial arts action, was a surprise hit, and spawned two sequels, Reloaded and Revolutions. A belated fourth film, Resurrections, was released in 2021.

Now, Puck reports that the Wachowskis are exercising their copyright termination rights, which means the rights to The Matrix, which they wrote and directed, will revert to them in 2029. That isn't guaranteed to happen; Paramount could contest the matter in court, arguing that the work was done under contract with the studio. Likewise, it could simply come to a new agreement with the Wachowskis that would allow them to continue to shepherd the franchise; Paramount recently did so with the heirs to the estate of Mario Puzo for the rights to The Godfather. While Lana directed and co-wrote Resurrections, neither Wachowski is behind the camera for the fifth Matrix film, which is being developed by Drew Goddard (Project Hail Mary).

Paramount is dealing with a number of copyright terminations at the moment, including The Shawshank Redemption, Beavis and Butt-Head, and ER. Victor Miller, who wrote the original Friday the 13th, exercised his rights before his death earlier this year, adding to the legal entanglements of that franchise; it was eventually resolved, with Miller earning a producer credit on the recent Crystal Lake miniseries. Recently, Michael Mann has filed to have the rights to his 1995 crime thriller Heat revert to him, which current rightsholder Regency Pictures is disputing.

Matt Damon Reveals Why Christopher Nolan Doesn’t Release Deleted Scenes

📅 Nov 18, 2026  |  Category: Entertainment  |  Read Source →

Christopher Nolan’s epic adaptation of The Odyssey is setting sail again for its home release. This Oscar contender is now available on Premium VOD, 4K, and DVD, ahead of its rerelease into theaters in IMAX 70mm on December 11, and in honor of this next journey, Collider’s Steve Weintraub had the opportunity to sit down and talk with stars Matt Damon and John Leguizamo.

This summer, Homer’s classic poem took over cinema, as Damon led his lost and weary crew in a voyage back to Ithaca as the unwavering Odysseus. And lest we forget, the film offered compelling performances by Anne Hathaway, Robert Pattinson, Tom Holland, Himesh Patel, Lupita Nyong’o, Jon Bernthal, and many others, as well as stunning sequences that filmed all across the world.

Don’t miss the full conversation in the video above, with a transcript provided below, where the pair discuss the possibility of 70mm ratio at home, the IMAX rerelease, and Nolan’s thoughts and approach to deleted scenes. They also discuss reading Nolan’s scripts for the first time, and why fans should check out the screenplays themselves to compare to the final cut.

COLLIDER: Let me just start with two quick things. Number one, I cannot wait to see Miami Vice [‘85].

I’m very excited. And number two, I probably am the only one who can say this to you, but I've seen Stuntnuts.

I think people are unprepared for you, Chris Hemsworth, and Sam Rockwell and the way you play…

DAMON: Ourselves. [Laughs] I had so much fun. I mean, I had a very brief cameo, but Chris and Sammy, their parts are bigger. Watching the dailies of those two guys, I don't think I've laughed that hard. They are so good together. So, I'm wondering what Matthew [Vaughn]'s going to do and when he's going to bring it out. But yeah, it needs people who've seen it kind of championing it together.

[The Odyssey] is coming out on home video, and I saw this in IMAX 70 millimeter, and it was, pardon my language, fucking insane. I don't think it's being released with the IMAX 70 ratio. Did you ask Chris, “Hey, can we release this in the IMAX 70 ratio so people can experience a version of it at home?”

DAMON: Oh, that's interesting. I'll ask him what his opinion is on that. I mean, I know they do it with all formats in mind. They usually center punch the actors so that they can go over and kind of meticulously frame everything after for the different formats. But I'll ask him if there is a theory behind releasing it in that format. Maybe they think maybe we'll give up too much of the screen. I don't know.

JOHN LEGUIZAMO: Or the faces get too small.

DAMON: I wonder if they're worried that the scale might miniaturize it too much. I'll ask him what he thinks. That's a good question.

I just think it would be nice if there was a version of that.

DAMON: A version of it. Yeah, yeah, yeah.

Because also, the way I see it is that there are so many people around the world who I saw online like, “I can't see it in IMAX 70.”

DAMON: I have friends who still call me, and they're like, “I haven't seen it because I really want to see it in an IMAX 70.”

LEGUIZAMO: They’ve got one shot coming up, right? December 7th. It goes back into IMAX 70.

Yeah, but there are a lot of people who don't live near a theater. That's the real issue.

A highly competitive Oscar season is underway. Which films are heading toward nominations?

Nolan is famous for not releasing deleted scenes, so I'm just curious, was there anything you shot that you're a little sad is never going to see the light of day?

DAMON: I mean, there's some, I’m sure, stuff that we shot that we've forgotten about. There was so much love and care and work put into every second that got photographed. Obviously, as is the case with most movies, if you put it all in, it's going to be five hours long, and you've got to tell the story. And look, this story, we came in at under three hours.

DAMON: That is a brisk pace for this story.

LEGUIZAMO: It’s 800 pages or whatever the poem is.

DAMON: It's so much to cover. And Oppenheimer was like that, too. He's really judicious about how he tells the story. But he takes on a lot, and you have to move through it. So, I don't know that there's anything. I trust he and Jennifer Lame to understand how the story needs to be told.

LEGUIZAMO: I mean, in other movies, I know exactly what scenes were cut and it really upset me, like The Menu or To Wong Foo or Carlito's Way, but not this. I don't recall. Did he use everything? I don't think he used everything.

DAMON: But he's incredible in the writing process in terms of how specific he is. It’ll be interesting because now his screenplays are available. They'll put them out. So, I'm sure you can get them online.

LEGUIZAMO: And this is his magnum opus. This script was such a phenomenal read. I had to be home to receive it. Nobody else was allowed to receive it, and it was cherry red, on shiny, gray slate ink.

DAMON: Yeah, with a flashlight. [Laughs] You needed a decoder ring. A Captain Crunch decoder ring. No, but for film students, I would definitely suggest buying a screenplay of his. At least the three movies I've done — Interstellar, Oppenheimer, and this one.

LEGUIZAMO: And Daniel Mendelsohn's version of The Odyssey. Read the book and the script and see the genius of his work.

DAMON: See the genius of the adaptation. But then look at the film versus the screenplay, and look at how close he is. I’ll never forget when I read Oppenheimer. He gave it to me on a Friday night, and I couldn't read it right away. We had people visiting from Australia. I said, “Chris, I'll read it first thing tomorrow morning.” And he said, “How long do you take to read?” I said, “I read it exactly the pace the movie will play,” because that's just always how I've read. I try to really see the movie as I'm reading it. And he goes, “What time are you going to start?” And I said, “I will start at 9 a.m.” And he said, “I will be here at noon.” And at 11:59, I am turning the last page of Oppenheimer, and the doorbell rings, and in he comes.

And that movie that I read, you can go read that screenplay, I'm telling you, and you watch Oppenheimer, and that's the film. And The Odyssey was like that, too. So there's not a lot of work. And in fact, he said, “Do you see anywhere, just writer to writer, where to cut anything cut?” He goes, “Because Oppenheimer's three hours.”

DAMON: He just said, “Do you have any ideas for me to noodle?” I said, “I really don't.” I said, “This is very lean at three hours.” But I go, “Why don't we shoot it, and then you can decide later?” And Chris goes, “I don't do that.” And I'm like, what director doesn't do that? But he was very serious. If he goes in to shoot it, the intention is that it will be in the movie.

LEGUIZAMO: So he's cutting as he’s shooting?

DAMON: He's cutting as he's writing and as he's shooting. And so, for as big as it is, I mean, I would be surprised if there's a lot on the floor…

The Odyssey is available on Premium VOD, 4K, and DVD now, and returns to theaters in IMAX 70mm on December 11.

Jahmyr Gibbs is on pace to break the NFL record for yards from scrimmage in a season

📅 Nov 18, 2026  |  Category: Entertainment  |  Read Source →

Detroit’s Jahmyr Gibbs hasn’t just been the most productive running back in the NFL this season. He may produce more yardage than any player in NFL history this season.

Through nine games this season, Gibbs has an NFL-high 1,000 rushing yards. Gibbs also has 384 receiving yards. That total of 1,384 yards from scrimmage leads the league, and with an average of 153.8 yards per game, Gibbs is on pace total 2,614 yards from scrimmage over a 17-game season.

That would break the NFL record, held by Chris Johnson of the Titans, who totaled 2,509 yards from scrimmage (2,006 rushing yards and 503 receiving yards) in 2009.

Gibbs has touched the ball an NFL-high 240 times this season, combining 199 carries and 41 catches. With that kind of workload, the biggest concern for the Lions will be whether he can stay healthy for 17 regular-season games and, the Lions hope, some playoff games after that.

But given how well Gibbs is playing, it would be hard for Dan Campbell and the Lions’ coaching staff to reduce his workload. He’s having a special season. He’s halfway to one of the greatest seasons any running back has ever had.

‘Hazara Town’ Review: A Restrained but Deeply Affecting Doc Portrait of an Afghan Refugee Family Living in Limbo

📅 Nov 18, 2026  |  Category: Entertainment  |  Read Source →

Khadim Dai's excellent IDFA competition premiere is emotionally acute and clear-eyed, even as it documents the plight of the filmmaker's own family.

A gauze of dust covers everything in the Pakistani desert city of Quetta, rendering just about everything the same shade of bleached, parched beige — an in-between color for an in-between place, where many residents await change with no clear timeline. In the city quarter of Hazara Town, named for over half a million residents from the ethnic Hazara community, displaced refugees from Afghanistan live in indefinite limbo: violently imperiled in their current sanctuary, but not yet granted asylum anywhere else. It’s a despairing holding pattern through which everyday life, with its more modest domestic challenges and occasional joys, nonetheless continues: Khadim Dai‘s patiently observed, intensely moving documentary “Hazara Town” sharply chronicles that simultaneous stasis and progress. Related Stories Microsoft Says 'No Evidence of Malicious Activity' in Global Outage of Outlook and Exchange Email as It Begins to Restore Services Outlook Outage: Microsoft's Email Service Goes Down Nationwide

The first feature-length film by the Afghan-born, Los Angeles-based Dai is a family affair, centered on the director’s own stranded relatives: his sister-in-law, Sedeqa; his two young nieces, Machid and Mitra; and their doting grandparents. All are stranded in Quetta since fleeing Afghanistan, while Dai’s brother Hussain has forged ahead, laying the groundwork for a new life in Canada. Following him is no simple matter, however. The asylum application process is slow and selective, and whole chapters of family life pass by as they wait to be reunited: Preteen Mitra, in particular, doesn’t remember much of her father’s presence at home, and communication by phone and video only partially bridges the gap.

Returning to Pakistan several years after his own departure, Dai embeds himself in the household, his camera capturing both the intimate daily routines of a splintered family — in particular, the growth of two girls around their father’s absence — and wider currents of fear and sorrow in the Hazara community, where people increasingly find themselves as unsafe from ethnic and religious persecution as they were in the homeland they fled. Targeted killings are disturbingly commonplace, the rising death toll sporadically flashing on the screen with no further commentary.

Dai’s camera records no violence, but instead spends much time in the local cemetery where the dead are buried with little ceremony — their portraits displayed for a time as a memorial before they, too, vanish. Deft, detailed sound design balances the lonely reverberations of the desert wind against the industrious tap-tap-tapping of local stonemasons at work on various grave markers: At least some in this stymied community have much to do. But the cemetery isn’t presented only as a place of grief: It’s also a gathering spot for people otherwise granted little social space, and in some of the film’s most poignant tableaux, children chatter and play amidst these humble resting places.

Home, though, remains the most secure of spaces in an otherwise stateless existence: With its modest interior courtyard and few windows to the outside world, Dai’s family’s residence feels significantly turned inward. Though Machid and Mitra both go to school, classroom freedoms are curbed as Pakistan hands over responsibility for refugee education to the Taliban; it’s within cramped domestic walls that the girls assert their evolving personalities and interests, rapidly maturing before our eyes.

Machid, a studious bookworm with a love of Judy Blume, dreams of richer education opportunities in the United States, while the rest of the family pins their hopes on Canada — sold to them via effortfully cheerful video missives from Hussain, marveling at differences in culture and weather. Good news does arrive from North America, but not for everyone, and so the family is further fragmented. Dai films each individual flight from the back window of the departing taxi, splashed with a bucket of water for good luck, the drips distorting one last look at those left behind in the rearview.

Also an accomplished cinematographer, Dai shoots these individually unremarkable episodes of daily survival — and occasional celebration — with a grace and sensitivity that cumulatively builds to something more than just diaristic in effect: “Hazara Town” is a quietly emphatic statement of presence for lives all too easily disappeared, or dismissed as statistics. And while it provides no tidy resolution to this collective narrative, there’s at least a temporary, heart-filling interlude of reunion, before life pulls in a multitude of directions: For the refugee family, there’s as much anguish in movement as in stillness, but a shred of hope too.

8 Funniest 'Far Side' Comics Starring Cowboys

📅 Nov 18, 2026  |  Category: Entertainment  |  Read Source →

Gary Larson, the comedy genius behind The Far Side comic strip, loved to turn things into their opposites. Serious, brilliant scientists were cast as childish dummies who drop everything when they hear an ice cream truck pass by. Simple-minded cows could be so smart that they plot the deaths of their owners. As for cowboys, the manly men of the Old West, Larson loved to portray them as meek and cowardly.

Sometimes he made them just plain weird, which Larson did with everything.

Here are eight of the funniest Far Side comics about cowboys, who are portrayed quite differently than they were in the typical Clint Eastwood or John Wayne movie.

This comic strip is a great example of Larson’s love of turning popular trends and tropes upside down. The adorable bunnies, armed with revolvers, have taken over the stagecoach with two Old West cowboys. The funniest part about the predicament, though, is the one cowboy’s concern for how this is going to look on their official report.

Being the fastest gunslinger in town was a point of pride for cowboys—or at least for the cowboys in the movies I’ve seen— so the idea of a gunman being so slow that he loses to a sloth is especially hilarious.

This comic strip feels like it’s a play on this classic Clint Eastwood movie line, "You've got to ask yourself one question: 'Do I feel lucky?'"

To be clear, this didn’t come from a Clint Eastwood Western. It’s actually from Dirty Harry, which is a movie where Eastwood plays a cop instead. Still, Eastwood’s strong ties to Western films make this line ripe for parody in a Western setting.

For the record, it seems that, in this comic strip, Bart has literally never been lucky in his life, having lost a leg, an eye, a hand, and having been shot through his hat many times. I mean, perhaps he’s lucky he hasn’t died, but it feels like being super lucky would have protected him from getting shot at all.

Tattling is never a tough-guy thing to do, which is why this comic is so funny. It shows one cowboy squealing on another, telling on him for having said a discouraging word. This, of course, is a play on the old folk song “Home on the Range,” which has the lyric “Where seldom is heard a discouraging word.”

This one is just incredibly weird, but still incredibly funny. These cowboys are comparing how tiny their horses are, which they keep in little jars. It is such a strange image, but that's where the comedy comes from.

Honestly, I would have loved to see this acted out by some famous western star like Clint Eastwood or John Wayne or someone like that.

This type of joke isn’t specific to cowboys, but it’s the kind of thing Larson did a lot with The Far Side, which was to take a common expression and interpret it in the most literal way possible. For the old cowboy threat “This town ain’t big enough for the both of us,” Larson portrayed two gunslingers in a tiny little town of dollhouse-sized buildings.

Another example of Larson being overly literal is this comic strip. It is a common trope in Westerns for a hero to ride off into the sunset. Only Larson could turn that into a charred cowboy who actually rode into the sun.

This one isn’t about opposites or about cowboys being weirdos, but it’s the funniest-ever Far Side comic about cowboys by virtue of being a shockingly dark joke. Only Gary Larson could make the punchline to a comic strip a pile of dead horses. It’s hard to imagine Charles Schulz or Jim Davis ever doing such a thing. But that’s what made The Far Side so funny, because it didn't follow the rules of other comic strips.

8-Episode Netflix Horror Series With 100% RT Rating Delivers A Masterclass In Building Tension

📅 Nov 18, 2026  |  Category: Entertainment  |  Read Source →

While conventional wisdom might claim that slow-burn horror is always more critically acclaimed than hardcore scares, one Netflix masterpiece with a flawless critical rating proves this isn’t strictly true. It can sometimes seem that, despite horror gaining a critical foothold in recent years, certain entries into the genre are still taken a lot more seriously by reviewers than others.

To be fair, this is partially a reasonable response. The Scream spinoff Scream: The Series was one of the franchise’s most divisive entries for years before 2026’s risible Scream 7, but this was mainly because the show abandoned the self-aware meta humor that made the franchise famous in the first place. Similarly, the fact that Peacock’s Friday the 13th prequel, Crystal Lake, received drastically better reviews than the rest of the franchise is most likely because the spinoff is simply far better than those earlier slasher movies.

That said, it is not unreasonable to note that horror stories which incorporate a metaphor about the protagonist’s trauma, no matter how tacked-on or perfunctory, often receive more credit than genre entries that simply revel in scaring their audience. Similarly, if Mike Flanagan’s successful Carrie reboot proved anything, it was that sympathetic, slow-burn horror typically fares better than jump-scare-fuelled shockfests. At least, until 2019’s Netflix hit Marianne is taken into consideration.

Co-written by future Cobweb creator Samuel Bodin alongside Quoc Dang Tran, Marianne sees Victoire Du Bois’s conflicted horror author Emma return to her small hometown to find out the truth behind the mysterious death of her childhood friend, Caroline. Soon, Emma realizes to her horror that the characters in her horror novels are seemingly starting to manifest in the real world, including a particularly odious evil witch.

This setup will likely sound familiar to anyone who has seen director John Carpenter’s criminally underrated In the Mouth of Madness, a riff on Lovecraft where Sam Neill’s troubled horror author gets trapped in his own fictional world. However, that classic movie, much like many screen adaptations of Lovecraft’s work, starts out slowly and only reveals the extent of its horror as In the Mouth of Madness reaches its wild finale.

In contrast, Marianne takes a huge risk that massively pays off when the show’s pilot features a slew of terrifying jump scares and gruesome scenes. Right from the off, Marianne makes it perfectly clear that, despite the popularity of movies like The Witch and Get Out at the time of the show’s release, this is no subtle, slow-burn horror story at all.

This seems like a terrible choice, since it opens Marianne up to accusations of focusing more on jump scares than character development and atmosphere. Ever since the classic 1990 It miniseries and Twin Peaks, most critically adored horror shows are patient slow burns like Mike Flanagan's Netflix shows The Haunting of Hill House and Midnight Mass, The Walking Dead franchise’s outings, or The Terror.

Occasionally, viewers will be blessed with a horror show that goes full-throttle from start to finish, like the infamously absurd American Horror Story, certain episodes of anthologies like Masters of Horror or Tales from the Crypt, or, in recent years, streaming successes like Brand New Cherry Flavor. However, these are few and far between, and much more likely to face criticism for starting strong but gradually losing steam or sense.

In contrast, Marianne’s decision to pair a fast and furious start with a slower, more atmospheric middle and a terrifying ending blends both approaches perfectly. It’s a pacing approach that is unique to this Netflix masterpiece, and one that means the 100% Rotten Tomatoes critical rating success of Marianne has rarely been matched in the seven years since the show debuted.

Elizabeth Debicki Thanks the People Who Opened Doors for Her in Her ELLE Women in Hollywood Speech

📅 Nov 17, 2026  |  Category: Entertainment  |  Read Source →

Elizabeth Debicki knows that no career is built alone. On Monday night, the actress accepted her honor at ELLE’s 2026 Women in Hollywood celebration, taking a moment to recognize the people who helped make her career possible.

After being introduced by Alexander Skarsgård, Debicki thanked her fellow honorees before reflecting on the support she has received throughout her journey.

“I really love to work. It is my great love,” she said. “And standing here tonight, I feel a really deep sense of gratitude for all the people—the small army of people—who have fought for me to be able to do my work.”

Debicki recalled how casting director Ronna Kress helped her secure an audition for Baz Luhrmann’s The Great Gatsby, which became her breakout film role. At the time, she was just 20 years old, had recently graduated from drama school in Australia, and had never done a screen test.

“Now, Baz, who is my fairy godfather, he fought very, very hard for me after I auditioned for him,” she explained. “But the only reason I was able to get that audition was because Ronna Kress watched the shoddy rookie tape I made back home and somehow managed to convince Baz to sit still long enough to watch it (which, if you know, you know is an actual miracle).”

She continued, “Ronna fought very, very hard for the studio to fly me out here to L.A.—I had never been here before—and to read for the part. And truly, that changed everything. That was really, really my door. And Ronna and Baz, they held that open for me.”

Debicki also praised the other women being recognized that evening: Julianne Moore, Penélope Cruz, Florence Pugh, Inde Navarette, Rosalind Eleazar, and Carrie Coon.

“In your work, your audiences are so privileged to witness your talent and your intelligence,” she said. “And this generosity in the way that you work, it makes us feel sane, and it makes us feel seen. So thank you, so, so much.”

Carrie Coon Speaks Out About Women’s Representation at ELLE’s Women in Hollywood Event

📅 Nov 17, 2026  |  Category: Entertainment  |  Read Source →

Carrie Coon had a question for her fellow Women in Hollywood honorees: “Can we start a studio?”

On Monday night, the actress took the stage at ELLE’s 2026 Women in Hollywood celebration in Los Angeles, where she called for women in the entertainment industry to come together and create more opportunities for themselves.

Coon was introduced by her Gilded Age co-star Louisa Jacobson, who celebrated her recent career achievements.

“Carrie is a crusader, always fighting tirelessly for artistic integrity in a landscape that seems to value high gloss and profit over honest, nuanced storytelling,” Jacobson said. “And if you know Carrie, you know that she is completely allergic to superficiality.”

In her acceptance speech, Coon reflected on the importance of women’s stories, drawing inspiration from feminist literature and End Times Fascism, a book by Naomi Klein and Astra Taylor.

“Patriarchy, capitalism...these are hierarchical systems of domination that rely on fear,” she said. “And in this paradigm, women and girls, like our natural resources, are a disposable commodity—and as such, according to philosopher Kate Manne—our stories are interchangeable and of equal power. But of course, they’re not. That’s not true. And we’re not a monolith.”

Coon went on to discuss the performances of her fellow honorees, arguing that their work represented something extraordinary. “In these women, I see the fight for the living world. I see moral clarity. I see imagination. I see courage,” she said. “And these are, in fact, the antidotes to fascism. That’s how important this storytelling is.”

Toward the end of her speech, Coon questioned how women in Hollywood could create lasting change beyond events celebrating their individual achievements.

“How do we forge our collective safety and solidarity in an ethic of communalism?” she asked. “Okay, so this is the part of the speech where I don’t actually have the answer to that question, but I make the appeal that nights like these shouldn’t be one-off nights.”

She concluded with the aforementioned proposition for her peers: “And so I’m going to start with a really stupid question, but I’m not afraid to ask a stupid question: Can we start a studio?”

Watch Coon’s full speech above and read her Women in Hollywood cover story here.

9 Classics Where the Audiobook Version Is Better

📅 Nov 17, 2026  |  Category: Entertainment  |  Read Source →

Audiobooks were technically born as far back as 1877, with Thomas Edison's invention of the phonograph. However, it wasn't until 1932 that the American Foundation for the Blind officially named them "Talking Books," introducing them as a medium for visually impaired people to enjoy literature as an alternative to Braille. Throughout the entire 20th century, and even well into the 21st, many classics have been turned into audiobooks so phenomenal that they're arguably even better than the original book.

Audiobooks and physical books are, of course, vastly different media that fulfill vastly different purposes, so comparing them can feel a bit like comparing apples to oranges. Still, sometimes, an audiobook is so good that it becomes entirely reasonable to say it provides a better experience than the original. These are audiobooks that anyone who enjoys classic literature should consider essential listening material. "Classic" is defined for the purposes of this list as any book that was originally published before the 21st century.

Folkloric vampires predate Bram Stoker's Dracula by hundreds of years. Inspired by such stories, as well as by Irish folklore and historical figures like Vlad the Impaler, Stoker wrote the 1897 classic that is nowadays widely remembered as the most foundational pillar of modern vampiric fiction. It's undoubtedly one of the best horror books of all time, which made the announcement of an original Audible audiobook-slash-audio-drama all the more worthy of skepticism.

In the end, however, Bram Stoker's Dracula ended up being one of the greatest horror audiobooks that readers have been treated to in years. With a stacked cast that includes the likes of Ella Purnell, Jonathan Bailey, and Aimee Lou Wood, the audiobook takes the classic epistolary novel and makes it feel even more immersive, cinematic, and well-paced. It's worth noting that it's an abridged audio drama rather than a full reading of the original novel, which is not ideal, but those interested in such a concept should love it.

Originally serialized between 1875 and 1877, Russian author Leo Tolstoy's Anna Karenina is widely regarded as one of the most foundational pieces of world literature in history, a must-read masterpiece whose daunting 1,000-page length shouldn't deter anyone from considering it essential reading. Since it was one of her favorite books of all time, Maggie Gyllenhaal agreed to narrate an Audible original audiobook that really does justice to one of the best epic books of all time.

Tolstoy's novel was such a monumental artistic achievement that the author was notoriously drained and exhausted by the whole experience by the time he was done, and reading such a vast book can itself also be a massive undertaking. With the raw immediacy and many emotional variations of Gyllenhaal's narration, listening to Anna Karenina can be a far more engaging and far less tedious experience than many may initially think. At over 30 hours long, it's definitely a time commitment, but oh so worth every minute.

Upon publication back in 1997, J. K. Rowling's Harry Potter and the Philosopher's Stone (published in the US as Harry Potter and the Sorcerer's Stone) was an immediate commercial and critical hit, kicking off one of the biggest multimedia franchises in history. The book's first audiobook version came out in 1999, and the performers varied by region. Whereas Jim Dale narrated the book in the US and Canada, it was Stephen Fry's voice that was heard in the British and international releases. If there is any one Harry Potter book that's better heard than read, it's Fry's performance of Philosopher's Stone.

As a whole, this is one of the most essential fantasy books to read before kicking the bucket, but there's nothing quite like hearing it through Fry's rich, velvety baritone voice. On the page, Philosopher's Stone is beautifully imaginative and cozy, but at times feels like an overly simple debut novel in terms of Rowling's prose. Fry elevates every word and makes the entire story feel like a wonderful fairy tale being told by the fire on a cold weekend night outdoors.

One of Stephen King's first novels was 1983's Pet Sematary, published before he had fully established and cemented himself as the true King of Horror. Fans had been begging for an audiobook for years, and once the project finally materialized, it was Michael C. Hall of Dexter fame who publisher Simon & Schuster picked as the right voice to narrate the story. The result is one of the best Stephen King audiobooks to date, and considering just how many great ones there are, that's saying a lot.

Pet Sematary was already absolutely legendary well before 2018, widely regarded by many—including King himself—as the author's most genuinely terrifying work. However, once the horror aspects really pick up steam after a relatively slow-burning first few pages, Hall makes it hit even harder. Getting an actor well-known for projects as death-related as Six Feet Under and Dexter to narrate a horror novel all about death and grief was not only brilliant meta-casting, but also hugely beneficial for the story as a whole.

Multiple books are almost always brought up when the endless debate of what "The Great American Novel" is gets brought up, but John Steinbeck's East of Eden is the most noteworthy. This family saga was likely key to Steinbeck being awarded the Nobel Prize in Literature in 1962, and it more than lives up to its reputation as one of the most perfect American books ever written. The first audiobook version of the classic ever recorded was narrated by respected character actor Richard Poe.

East of Eden was already widely recognized as one of the most perfectly written masterpieces of all time, and it's definitely a must-read, but there's something about the way Poe complements the narrative that makes his version arguably even more unforgettable. Steinbeck wrote East of Eden in such a sweeping, almost Biblical voice that it almost feels like the narration of a legend, and Poe's resonant yet deeply human voice allows that narration style to feel all the more intimate and personal.

The book that earned Harper Lee a Pulitzer Prize was 1960's To Kill a Mockingbird, one of the most essential and widely discussed American classics of the 20th century. Though certain elements of its narrative are undeniably dated by modern standards, its heart will forever be in the right place, with its critiques of racial prejudice and systemic injustice being as timely and hard-hitting today as they were back in the '60s. In 2014, legendary actress Sissy Spacek was tasked with narrating the audiobook version of the story.

The reasons why this is one of those great books that are even better as audiobooks are aplenty, and even though the Pulitzer-winning original remains absolutely foundational, Spacek brings a level of nuance and authenticity to the book that feels like a much-welcome sense of modernity. The actress' beautifully melodic Texan drawl makes the small-town Alabama setting feel all the more evocative and atmospheric, and the way she flawlessly captures Scout's personality as both a child and an adult is incredible.

Stephen King's 22nd book was 1986's It, still remembered by many as the author's best and most iconic work to date. At over 1,100 pages in length, it's an absolute brick of a horror novel, but its epic scope still works marvelously all these many years later. To narrate the 2016 audiobook, Simon & Schuster decided to go for an actor who already had a pre-existing tie to the Stephen King universe: Steven Weber, who played Jack Torrance in the 1997 three-part miniseries version of The Shining.

It was already one of those horror books where every chapter is perfect—something quite admirable for such a thick novel—but Weber took that literary doorstop and somehow turned it into an even bigger, more immersive 45-hour juggernaut of an audiobook. The actor gives each of the Losers a distinct voice both in their child and adult versions, and he goes all in on Pennywise without ever teetering too far into distracting camp. Perfectly paced and intense to the core, It is a must-listen-to audiobook through and through.

American poet, essayist, and civil rights activist Maya Angelou was a towering figure in modern American literature, completely reshaping how Black American identity was expressed in the written word. The first of her seven published memoirs, 1969's I Know Why the Caged Bird Sings, broke new ground and became one of the first major autobiographies by an African American woman to achieve global bestseller status. When an audiobook version was released in 2011, it was Angelou herself who narrated it.

Taking one of the best memoirs of all time and turning it into an audiobook worthy of its legacy was always going to be a tough task, so who better to achieve it than the author herself? By having Angelou narrate her own story with her unmistakably theatrical contralto voice, I Know Why the Caged Bird Sings feels even more honest and intimate than it already does on the page, balancing poignancy and humor to absolute perfection.

Well before he wrote what many still consider the greatest and most influential fantasy epic of the 20th century, J. R. R. Tolkien published the fantasy children's classic The Hobbit, almost inarguably the most perfect fantasy novel of the 1930s. There are multiple audiobook versions of The Hobbit available, but one reigns supreme as perhaps the audiobook that most elevates the experience of the original text: the 2020 version narrated by Andy Serkis.

It's easily one of the best audiobooks of all time, with Serkis not only delivering a much-expected powerhouse performance as Gollum, but also giving each of the other characters a distinct voice that makes them even more memorable than they are on paper. The Hobbit is also filled with songs and poems, and Serkis brings them to life in a way that reading the physical book simply can't match. In Serkis' voice, The Hobbit really does feel like the wonderful myth that it was always meant to be.

Brian Flores: Vikings plan to rotate players in order to deal with altitude in Mexico City

📅 2026-11-17  |  Category: Entertainment  |  Read Source →

The 49ers are spending the week in Colorado in order to acclimate to the altitude at which their Week 11 game in Mexico City will be played. The Vikings are not.

But the Vikings have a plan for ensuring that their players are ready for the conditions.

“It’s a tough place to play,” Vikings defensive coordinator Brian Flores told reporters on Tuesday regarding the game in Mexico. “I think it’s certainly taxing, you know, I think you’re — I think as a staff, we’re already talking about, ‘Hey, we’re gonna have to rotate these guys and get, you know, everybody’s gonna have to be ready to play.’ And so, yeah, I mean, it’s certainly something that we’re going to have to deal with.

“But there’s always, you know, elements that we’re dealing with, whether it’s cold or rain or things like that. Obviously, we’re indoors, so we don’t deal with much of that. But that’s the environment we’re gonna be in. I think we prepare them to play in that environment, but I’m not gonna be silly and say, ‘Hey, everybody, hey, you’ve got to go in there for 12 straight plays.’ I mean, we’re not doing that.

“So we’ll have rotations, we’ll have a plan in place. Inevitably, we’re gonna have to fight through some of that. It’s not gonna be your average game where, you know, altitude, you just have — you literally have less oxygen. So we’ve got to do a great job as a staff of making sure — watching guys, and making sure we got guys in and out, you know, as much as we can.”

His broader message to the defense is clear: “We’re gonna need everybody.”

Regardless of the plan to rotate players, the question becomes whether the 49ers will be able to rely more heavily on their best players because they’re properly acclimated to the thinner air. The Vikings are choosing a different strategy, and it’s going to create an obvious apples to apples comparison between the approaches that the two teams have taken — especially if the 49ers players seem to be fresh and the Vikings players seem to be gassed.

Gael Cameron Says Her ‘Below Deck’ Romance With Nathan Gallagher Was Abusive: ‘I Am Still Scared’

📅 Nov 17, 2026  |  Category: Entertainment  |  Read Source →

Almost a year after Below Deck star Nathan Gallagher was arrested for domestic abuse in Australia — and 12 weeks after the news went public — Gallagher’s former partner and cast member Gael Cameron is finally speaking out about their relationship.

Cameron broke her silence on an episode of the podcast We Need To Talk, hosted by Paul C. Brunson, where she publicly accused Gallagher of abuse for the first time and described a toxic relationship that’s left her in fear to this day.

“I know that he is not afraid to do anything to throw me under. He’s not afraid to lie… He’s always had this idea that he has ownership or control over me,” Cameron said tearfully. “I still feel that. And I am still scared of him. And that’s why I’ve not said anything, even though he’s thrown all these accusations out there.”

Gallagher and Cameron met while filming Season Nine of Below Deck: Mediterranean in 2023, both working on the superyacht’s deck team. Over three seasons, their relationship evolved from flirty co-workers to welcoming a son together last June. But this August — halfway through a season where Gallagher struggled to juggle his bosun duties and be a present father — news broke of Gallagher’s December 2025 arrest on domestic violence charges. According to Australian police, Gallagher attacked Cameron at her apartment in Sydney, resulting in his arrest on Dec. 27, 2025. During the physical altercation, Gallagher “placed both of his hands around the victim’s neck applying enough pressure which made her unable to breathe,” according to police records. He was charged with assault occasioning actual bodily harm, common assault, and intentionally choking a person without consent.

Although Gallagher previously told Rolling Stone he had proof that would clear his name, he failed to appear at a mandated court hearing in September. As a result, he was convicted by default on charges of assault. Australian authorities issued a warrant for his arrest, noting Gallagher was suspected to have fled the country. Bravo has since announced it has no plans to work with him.  Editor’s picks The 250 Greatest Albums of the 21st Century So Far The 100 Best TV Episodes of All Time The 500 Greatest Albums of All Time 100 Best Movies of the 21st Century

Both Bravo and Gallagher declined to comment further to Rolling Stone.

During the podcast episode, Cameron charts the timeline of her relationship with Gallagher, including the behind-the-scenes struggles that kept her silent and afraid. Cameron first met Gallagher while filming Season Nine of Below Deck: Mediterranean in 2023. At the time, she was dating another man but began a friendship with Gallagher. Their working relationship was often “playfully flirty” when the cameras were rolling, but they didn’t start seeing each other until filming wrapped.

In 2023, the two were in a long-distance relationship, but Cameron said there were early signs of anger issues in Gallagher. When they were able to meet up, Cameron described his “love bombing,” showering her with gifts and affection. When she found out she was pregnant, Gallagher convinced her to keep the baby — the first of many moves which she claims he did in order to isolate her.

“If I ever said, ‘I don’t know if I can do this. I don’t think it’s the right time,’ He would make me feel very bad about myself. He would make me feel very guilty and just terrible about myself,” she said. “I have quite a conservative mum. I couldn’t talk to anyone. So he was kind of my only sounding board because I was so afraid to tell anyone.”

But the pregnancy only aggravated Gallagher’s behavior, according to Cameron. She said that she spent close to “90 percent” of her pregnancy in tears, often feeling like any small mistake would trigger Gallagher’s anger. In one instance, she describes that while drunk, Gallagher jumped out of a moving car rather than talk to her. Once a social worker told her the relationship was “unsafe,” Cameron began reevaluating if they could stay together long term. “I’d be told to shut the fuck up. I’d be called a bitch, a slut, and all these horrible things,” she says. “There were multiple times when I was like ‘No no no, this cannot continue,’ because my son cannot grow up thinking that this is how you speak to women.” But then Below Deck came to call.  Related Content Hayden Panettiere's Mother Blames Brian Hickerson For Star’s Death Kid Rock's Nashville Bar Sued After Bouncers Allegedly Beat Patron Who 'Tapped' Guitar Lawsuit Accusing Selena Gomez and Wondermind Co-Founders of Fraud Is Dismissed Priscilla Presley Scores Partial Win Over Fraud Suit, But Claims About Lisa Marie’s Death Can Stand

When Gallagher flew to Croatia to film Below Deck: Mediterranean Season 11, Cameron didn’t initially want to go. She appeared on the series as a guest star, and lived off of the boat with her infant son. But she believes Gallagher pushed her to come to Croatia as another form of control. “It felt as though he was very worried about who I spoke to or who I was around when he wasn’t around,” she says. “There was this element of control that he would lose over me if I wasn’t in the same country as him.” She also describes incidents during heated fights, when Gallagher would record on his phone, pretending that she was striking him or hitting him unprovoked. Things came to a head on Dec. 27, 2025, when Cameron said Gallagher choked her while their son was in her arms, which led to Gallagher’s arrest and subsequent conviction. Cameron appeared visibly emotional when she recounted the night.

“When I was in the bathroom, and he had his hands around my neck and attempted to lift, my son was in my arms. All I could think is, ‘If I fall, so does he.’ From that moment, I should have known there’s no return,” she says. “When he had me pinned down and he was striking me on the jaw, I actually sat there and I asked if he was going to kill me. There was a moment where I actually asked that question. The reason I say this is because if things had not gotten that bad, I would never have gotten out. According to Cameron, she has not spoken to Gallagher or been in contact since he fled the country. But she says that his departure has changed her life for the better.

“Him leaving was the biggest weight off my shoulders. All of a sudden, I could have my family over to help me look after my son when I went to work. I could breathe again,” she says. “I had been living this hell. So life felt so bright.”

Billy Bob Thornton's A Simple Plan Is Now Streaming Free On Tubi

📅 Nov 17, 2026  |  Category: Entertainment  |  Read Source →

Katrina joined ScreenRant as a news writer in 2024, covering box office, expert interviews, exclusives, and new releases. Her work has appeared in CBR, All About Jazz, Rising Artists, and numerous other publications. She has an MFA in Creative Writing and has published more than 20 books. She currently writes for ScreenRant’s movie features team.

Billy Bob Thornton isn't a stranger to the thriller genre. His breakthrough came in 1992, when he co-wrote and starred in the critically acclaimed crime thriller One False Move. Since then, he has received three Academy Award nominations in his career. And now one of his best movies - A Simple Plan - is streaming for free.

He wrote and directed the 1996 thriller Sling Blade and also starred in the lead role, Karl Childers, which earned him his first Oscar nomination as an actor. His script won the Academy Award in the Best Adapted Screenplay category that year.

While Sling Blade may have been a career-defining project for Thornton, two years later, he starred in an acclaimed but lesser-known crime thriller that led to his third Oscar nomination.

Directed by Sam Raimi, A Simple Plan features Thornton as Jacob, the odd older brother of Hank Mitchel, who discovered 4.4 million dollars and a plane with their friend Lou. The movie follows the three as they decide to keep the money.

Adapted from Scott B. Smith's novel of the same name, A Simple Plan's premise is very straightforward, but the escalation comes as a surprise. The movie is brilliantly paced, with the first half loaded with psychological tension and suspense and the second half filled with action and consequences.

Thornton's character, Jacob, is layered and tragic. He first appeared to be somewhat goofy and greedy, fast to side with Lou who proposed on taking the 4.4 million dollars. His brother Hank appeared to be the more law-abiding person, who was against stealing. However, as the film progresses, viewers will soon find the roles reversed.

A Simple Plan received high praise from critics, including Roger Ebert, when it came out in 1998. In addition to Thornton's Best Supporting Actor nomination, Smith's script also received a nomination for Best Adapted Screenplay. It's hard to imagine another crime thriller matching the brilliance of Sling Blade, but A Simple Plan comes very close.

Billy Bob Thornton's performance is nuanced and deliberate. He makes Jacob seem like the least likable and least intelligent brother at first, but there are moments where the characters manage to surprise viewers. Jacob has depth and a conscience, which makes his tragic ending heartbreaking.

What's even more uncanny is how much the Landman actor seems to reveal about Jacob and Hank's father through his delivery of dialogue. He peels back the layers of an easily overlooked, normal-day guy and reveals the person underneath, who is good-natured, kind, observant, and almost too disillusioned and witty for his own good. Even though Jacob isn't the lead character, he manages to steal all the spotlight.

A Simple Plan officially joined Tubi's free streaming platform in November, in addition to Pluto TV, Paramount+, and YouTube subscriptions. Despite the 1998 neo-noir crime thriller being a masterpiece, it's often overlooked in both Sam Raimi and Billy Bob Thornton's filmographies.

Several factors have contributed to its lack of hype. Known for directing horror and action movies such as Evil Dead and the original Spider-Man trilogy, Raimi has a kinetic visual style. The film's slow-burning, atmospheric style takes a detour from the director's usual high-energy, flashy aesthetics.

It also came out two years after Fargo, which established a cultural touchstone by blending dark comedy with a snowbound Midwestern crime aesthetic. A Simple Plan is often considered a darker and bleaker cousin of the Coen brothers' film. While A Simple Plan was critically acclaimed, the movie wasn't a huge box office hit. It struggled in theaters and grossed only $16 million.

Google commemorates Robert Pattinsons James Bond casting with a sneaky Easter egg

📅 Nov 17, 2026  |  Category: Entertainment  |  Read Source →

Robert Pattinson's casting as James Bond has left Google feeling shaken, not stirred.

Following the announcement that Pattinson would be our next 007, Google introduced a new Bond-themed Easter egg. If you search up Pattinson's name, the search results shake a little, and a small twist of lemon falls from the top of the screen to rest daintily on Pattinson's head.

The Easter egg is a nod to James Bond's classic cocktail order: a vodka martini, shaken, not stirred. Google has essentially turned Pattinson into the cocktail he'll be ordering onscreen.

Pattinson's casting as Bond came after weeks of speculation and reported screen tests for potential Bond candidates, including Callum Turner, Jack Lowden, Josh O'Connor, and Nicholas Hoult.

Pattinson will star in the as-yet unnamed 26th Bond film, the first since Daniel Craig took his last bow in 2021's No Time To Die. Dune helmer Denis Villeneuve is set to direct. The pair have also worked together on the upcoming Dune: Part Three. Villeneuve and Pattinson's Bond film does not yet have a release date.

Bond won't be the first franchise Pattinson has led to be commemorated with a Google Easter egg. In 2022, tied to The Batman's release, if you searched terms such as "Bruce Wayne" or "Gotham City," a yellow Bat-Signal would appear alongside the results. Clicking on it turned the page into a moody Gotham night, complete with thunder, the Bat-Signal, and Batman swinging around the screen.

Plus, if you search up "Twilight" now, you'll be treated to a small line of scrolling text that reads "hoa hoa hoa season," flanked by falling leaf emojis. The Easter egg references Twilight fans' loving nickname for fall, drawn from the so-called "hoa hoa hoa" vocals of Blue Foundation's "Eyes on Fire" from the Twilight soundtrack. Netflix's New Psychological Thriller Series With Near Perfect RT Audience Score Defines A Major Era

📅 Nov 17, 2026  |  Category: Entertainment  |  Read Source →

Netflix's new psychological thriller series that has become a global streaming sensation comes to cement a 2026 trend and define a major era for the streamer. Netflix has always had success when it comes to thrillers, which is no surprise, as the first Netflix Original series to make waves, both in terms of viewership and awards, was Kevin Spacey's House of Cards.

The political thriller series has since ended, but it went on a successful 6-season run, being a long-running hit for the streamer. Over the years, Netflix's hall of thriller series has been upgraded with a wide array of shows, including political, spy, legal, medical, and many other thriller subgenres, with all of those shows fitting Netflix's patented binge release model.

Penn Badgley's You, at five seasons, Money Heist, also at five seasons, The Diplomat, recently renewed for season 5, and others have gotten to go on long runs at the streamer. In fact, one of Netflix's Originals with the most seasons is the Spanish thriller Elite, at a whopping eight seasons. Now comes a new psychological genre series to shake things up on the streaming service.

The Body is Netflix's latest psychological thriller series, having released all of its eight episodes on the streamer on November 11. It has instantly become a global sensation, and the show taps into a pocket of the genre that has led to some of the most exciting 2026 Netflix releases, defining a major era for the streaming service in comparison to one of the years best Netflix shows so far.

The Body taps into the same supernatural psychological thriller territory of the Netflix show Something Very Bad Is Going to Happen, which was released on the streaming service back on March 26, also with eight episodes. The latter focused on Camila Morrone's Rachel, a bride who discovers that not only is she marrying into a creepy rich family when she goes to their winter home in the woods with her groom, but also has to deal with a dark curse that could take her life on the upcoming wedding day. Something Very Bad Is Going to Happen topped Netflix's charts.

According to data from FlixPatrol, Something Very Bad Is Going to Happen was the 47th most-watched TV show on Netflix for the first semester of 2026, racking up 20.3 million views. The Body now comes to take its place as the premier Netflix supernatural psychological thriller series of 2026. At the time of writing, the new show is the most popular on the streamer worldwide, according to FlixPatrol. On top of that, The Body has surpassed Something Very Bad Is Going to Happen with both critics and viewers alike. On Rotten Tomatoes, it boasts an 87% critics' score and a 97% audience score.

Both of those are higher than what Morrone's show achieved, as Something Very Bad Is Going to Happen stands at a high 84% critics' score, but only a decent 63% audience score. The Body's supernatural twist comes from how a group of teenaged schoolgirls perform a sacrifice ritual, as a joke, during Halloween to win their dance competition, which the Prince of Darkness seemingly hears, leading to prophetic visions and all hell breaking loose.

Talking to What’s On Netflix, creator Quinn Shephard confirmed there are plans for where the show could go next if Netflix were to renew the new psychological drama. According to the creative, "There are some Season 2 ideas percolating in my brain and in Gina’s [Gammell] brain." If that were to happen, it would cement another way in which The Body beats Something Very Bad Is Going to Happen to define Netflix's new era of genre shows, as that thriller was designed as a limited series, not having any concrete return plans right now. As such, The Body can rise above it.
